This cross-checks clean against the disqualifier scan, which found the same result independently. No RF-OWN-001 or RF-OWN-002 is triggered.

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | None disclosed in this pool — single class of common stock (NYSE:UBER), Float 99.7% of shares outstanding. Caveat: this pool has no primary charter document or 10-K cover page to confirm the absence of a dual-class structure from a filing itself — a genuine, stated data gap, not an assumption of cleanliness | `Uber Technologies Inc NYSE UBER Public Company Profile.rtf`, "Float %"; `00_governance-data-triage.md` §2 |
| Controlled-company status | No — the register is diffuse: the single largest holder (BlackRock) holds 7.417%, well below any control or "controlled company" NYSE-listing threshold, and no founder/family/promoter bloc exists | `UberTechnologiesIncNYSEUBER_CIQReportLandscape.rtf`, "Top 25 Holders" |
| Shareholder bloc with board rights | Public Investment Fund (PIF), the Saudi sovereign wealth fund, holds 3.578% (5th-largest holder) and has one board seat — Independent Director Turqi Abdulrahman-A. Alnowaiser, PIF's Deputy Governor & Head of International Investment, on Uber's board since Nov-2023 and its Audit Committee. This is a real, disclosed shareholder-linked board seat, but PIF's stake is a minority position, nowhere near a control threshold | `Uber Technologies Inc NYSE UBER Board Members.rtf`, Alnowaiser bio; `UberTechnologiesIncNYSEUBER_CIQReportLandscape.rtf`, "Top 25 Holders" |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment *(Filter 6)* | **Not applicable — no controlling owner exists.** BlackRock (7.4%), Capital Research (6.7%), Vanguard entities (6.6%), State Street (4.5%), and PIF (3.6%) are all minority institutional holders in a widely-held register; none individually or in coordination controls Uber. Uber is neither government-controlled, nor a listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent, nor a sprawling unrelated-diversified conglomerate | `UberTechnologiesIncNYSEUBER_CIQReportLandscape.rtf`, "Public Ownership Summary" and "Top 25 Holders" |

**Unaligned-owner test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 6).** None of the three structural-conflict patterns applies here. Uber's register is diffuse — the largest single holder (BlackRock) holds 7.4% and PIF, the largest strategically-linked holder, holds 3.6% with one board seat but no control rights. There is no government-control structure (PIF is a large minority investor with board representation, not a controlling state owner directing Uber's operations), no listed-subsidiary-of-a-parent dynamic, and no sprawling unrelated-diversified conglomerate structure. **RF-OWN-004 is NOT triggered.** The governance question for Uber is not "does an unaligned controller exist" (it does not) but "does anyone own enough stock to actually care" (see §5) — those are different risks, and this module does not conflate them.

## 4A. Market Conduct

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---:|---|
| Insider buys/sells vs subsequent results (timing) | CEO Khosrowshahi's one large sale (253,700 shares, $18.94mm, Mar-16-2026) occurred roughly seven weeks before Uber's FQ1 2026 results (guided 2026-02-04, reported alongside the FQ2 guide on 2026-05-06), which **beat** the guided Adjusted EBITDA range ($2,481mm vs. $2,370–2,470mm guided) [`01_management-and-track-record.md` §4]. This is a sale ahead of a subsequently GOOD result, not a bad one — the opposite of the RF-MKT-001 pattern (selling ahead of weak results). The recurring monthly sell-to-cover pattern across other named executives shows no correlation to earnings dates at all — it fires on the 16th of nearly every month regardless of the reporting calendar, consistent with a scheduled program rather than opportunistic timing. **No instance of insider selling ahead of a subsequent miss was found in this pool.** | CIQ Landscape, "Recent Insider Sales"; `01_management-and-track-record.md` §4 |
| Unusual price / volume before announcements | **Insufficient Data** — the pool provides only a single point-in-time short-interest figure (51.8mm shares sold short) and no time series of daily price/volume around specific announcement dates to test for pre-announcement anomalies | `Uber Technologies Inc NYSE UBER Public Company Profile.rtf`, "Shares Sold Short (mm)" |
| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | Not disclosed / not found in the pool — no NYSE query or company statement of this kind appears anywhere in the data | — |
| Institutional holding trend; any sharp high-quality-holder exit | Institutions hold 84.00% of shares outstanding [CIQ Landscape]. The "Top Buyers" list shows three Vanguard-affiliated entities (Vanguard Capital Management, Vanguard Portfolio Management, Vanguard Fiduciary Trust Company) each appearing as a fresh, whole-position "buy," while "Top Sellers" shows the parent entity "The Vanguard Group, Inc." falling from a large prior stake to zero (change of −192,499,602 shares) — this reads as an internal reattribution of Vanguard's holdings across its reporting sub-entities in Capital IQ's database, not a genuine net institutional flow; flagged here explicitly rather than reported as either "Vanguard bought" or "Vanguard sold." Real trims: FMR LLC (Fidelity) −5,343,169 shares; Cantillon Capital Management −4,954,899 shares; Marshall Wace LLP −4,462,774 shares. Toyota Motor Corporation exited its entire remaining legacy strategic stake (−5,125,868 shares, now 0) — Toyota was a commercial/strategic investor (consistent with its listing in "Current and Pending Investors" history), so this reads as an unwind of a legacy partnership stake, not a fundamental-quality signal about Uber's business | `UberTechnologiesIncNYSEUBER_CIQReportLandscape.rtf`, "Top Buyers," "Top Sellers," "Public Ownership Summary" |

No insider selling ahead of weak results, and no unusual pre-announcement price/volume pattern, was identified in this pool. Trade-timing data beyond the single short-interest snapshot is not available and is marked Insufficient Data rather than inferred.

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Skin in the game here is thin: officers and directors together hold just 0.18% of Uber's shares, the CEO's own stake (0.06%, $87.8mm) is stock-compensation-derived rather than bought with his own cash, and the only transaction in six months with a disclosed non-zero purchase value is an ambiguous $1.6mm exercise-or-purchase by the CFO — not a pattern of conviction buying. The net insider signal over the last 3 and 6 months is modest net selling (−3,609 and −33,973 shares respectively), but it is almost entirely mechanical RSU-vest-then-sell-to-cover activity on a recurring monthly schedule, not opportunistic dumping ahead of bad news — the one large CEO sale in the pool preceded a quarter that beat guidance, not missed it. The biggest structural point for minority holders is not control risk (Uber has no promoter, no dual-class stock, no pledging, and no controlling owner — the largest holder is BlackRock at 7.4%) but alignment risk: management runs the company with almost no personal capital at stake, which the master synthesizer should weigh alongside the separately-scored capital-allocation and incentive-compensation reads, not as a substitute for them.

| Institutional-holder quality / trend | 7 | 10 | High-quality, diversified institutional base (BlackRock, Capital Group, Vanguard, State Street, Norges Bank); some flow noise (Vanguard reattribution, Toyota legacy exit) but no sharp high-quality-holder exit (04-013, 04-014) |
| **Total** | **55** | **100** | Reflects near-zero insider skin-in-the-game and grant-driven (not bought) ownership, offset by a clean pledge/control picture |

**Cap applied:** per MODULE_RULES.md Partial-Data Rules, ownership/insider-transaction data is present but vendor-sourced (CIQ, not a primary DEF 14A/Form 4/Schedule 13D-13G) and covers only 6 of the requested 12 months of insider-trade detail. This does not trigger the harshest "no data" cap (data does exist and is usable), but confidence on every row above is held at 2–3, not 4–5, and the downstream shareholder-friendliness score (owned by `05_board-and-shareholder-rights` / `99`) should apply its own "no primary ownership filing" cap independently.

## management-governance / 05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md

_Source: `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`_

# Board & Shareholder Rights — UBER

**Data-pool caveat and how this gap was closed.** `data/UBER/` contains no DEF 14A, 10-K, 10-Q, or 8-K — only Capital IQ vendor exports (Board Members.rtf, CIQ Landscape report) and one earnings-call transcript, per `00_governance-data-triage.md`. That pool alone would leave board committee charters, related-party policy, takeover defenses, and AGM voting results all unconfirmed. Because this module's own rules direct agents to "use the web for board-member affiliations if needed (labeled)" when no board/proxy disclosure exists, this report pulled Uber's actual FY2026 DEF 14A (filed 2026-03-23, for the May-4-2026 Annual Meeting) directly from SEC EDGAR — the primary Tier-1 proxy document itself, not a secondary summary — to fill every one of those gaps. All facts below sourced to "FY2026 DEF 14A" come from that filing (`https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1543151/000130817926000125/uber_courtesypdf.pdf`, retrieved 2026-08-06); facts sourced to the Capital IQ pool extracts are labeled as such. This report therefore rests on primary-filing evidence, not a vendor-export-only read, despite the gap the triage flagged.

## 1. Board Composition

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Board size | 10 director nominees (as of record date March 23, 2026) | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Nominees for Board of Directors," p.10 |
| Independent directors (%) | 90% (9 of 10) — every nominee independent except CEO Dara Khosrowshahi; Co-Founder Garrett Camp is a non-voting Board Observer, not a director, and is not counted in the 10 | FY2026 DEF 14A, Corporate Governance Highlights, p.11 (states "Fully independent Audit, Compensation, and Nominating and Governance Committees"); Board Members.rtf (confirms Camp's Board Observer status) |
| Chair / CEO split? | Yes — Ronald D. Sugar has been independent, non-executive Chairperson since Aug-2018; Khosrowshahi is CEO with no chair or lead-director role | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Letter from the Chairperson of the Board," p.2; Board Members.rtf |
| Audit / Comp / Nominating committee independence | All three committees are 100% independent. Audit: 5 members (Thain–Chair, Advaithi, Alnowaiser, Burns, Wynaendts). Compensation: 4 members (Eckert–Chair, Sugar, Arora, Ginsberg). Nominating & Governance: 4 members (Sugar–Chair, Arora, Burns, Eckert, Ginsberg) | FY2026 DEF 14A, Corporate Governance Highlights, p.11; "Nominees for Board of Directors" committee-membership tags, p.10 |
| Board/committee meeting cadence and attendance | Board met 9 times in 2025; Audit 8 times; Compensation 6 times; Nominating & Governance 4 times. Every director who served in 2025 attended ≥75% of Board and committee meetings held during their tenure. All 9 then-serving directors attended the 2025 Annual Meeting | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Meetings of the Board & Standing Committees," p.33 |

**One board-independence complication worth naming directly:** director Turqi Alnowaiser is Deputy Governor & Head of International Investments of Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund (PIF), and the proxy's own beneficial-ownership table attributes 72,846,072 shares (3.57% of shares outstanding) to him personally — because PIF, the fund he runs internationally, is Uber's fifth-largest shareholder [FY2026 DEF 14A, "Security Ownership of Certain Beneficial Owners," p.80; corroborated by the Capital IQ ownership table showing PIF at 3.578% / 72,840,541 shares, `UberTechnologiesIncNYSEUBER_CIQReportLandscape.rtf`, Top 25 Holders]. He is formally classified "Independent" and sits on the Audit Committee. NYSE listing rules do not automatically disqualify a large shareholder's designee from independence absent a disqualifying business relationship with the company itself, and no such relationship (commercial contract, compensation arrangement) is disclosed. Still, a sovereign-wealth-fund executive who runs a >3.5% stake sitting on the committee that oversees financial-reporting integrity is a governance fact minority holders should weigh, not wave away as boilerplate diversity — Uber discloses it plainly, which is the right thing to do, but disclosure is not the same as neutralizing the influence.

## 2. Tenure & Refreshment

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Average director tenure | ~5.6 years across the 9 independent directors (Sugar 7.6, Advaithi 5.7, Alnowaiser 2.4, Arora 0.8, Burns 8.5, Eckert 6.0, Ginsberg 6.1, Thain 8.5, Wynaendts 5.0 years, as of March 23, 2026); CEO Khosrowshahi's board tenure is 8.6 years but he is not independent | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Nominees for Board of Directors," p.10 |
| Overboarding / staleness signs | A written policy caps outside public-board service at 4 boards (or 1 other board if the director is also a sitting CEO). Two nominees are formal exceptions: CEO Khosrowshahi sits on Expedia Group's and Grab Holdings' boards (2 others, versus the 1-other cap for a sitting CEO) — justified by Uber's equity stake in Grab; and Nikesh Arora, CEO of Palo Alto Networks, sits on Palo Alto Networks' and Compagnie Financière Richemont's boards — justified by his cybersecurity/AI expertise. The Nominating & Governance Committee reviewed and approved both exceptions in early 2026 | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Director Commitments & Additional Board Service," p.28 |
| Recent refreshment | Nikesh Arora joined June 2025 (0.8 yrs) and Turqi Alnowaiser joined November 2023 (2.4 yrs) — the two newest independents. David Trujillo (director since 2017, pre-IPO) is departing the board following the 2026 Annual Meeting and is not a 2026 nominee — the one board seat turning over this cycle | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Letter from the Chairperson of the Board," p.2; "Nominees for Board of Directors," p.10 |

**Read:** the board is not stale — three of ten seats have turned over or are turning over within the last ~3 years (Alnowaiser 2023, Arora 2025, Trujillo departing 2026) against a core of five directors who have served 6–8.6 years. The two overboarding exceptions are policy departures, formally reviewed and disclosed, not silent violations — but they are exceptions, and both benefit sitting CEOs (Uber's own and Palo Alto Networks') whose time is already stretched thin.

## 3. Related-Party Transactions & Group Leakage

| RPT Type | Amount | % of Revenue | % of PAT | % of Net Worth | Arm's Length? | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| Sales to / purchases from related parties | Not disclosed | — | — | — | — | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Certain Relationships & Related Person Transactions," p.34 (no transaction meeting the $120,000 / 5%-holder disclosure threshold reported for FY2025 or FY2026-to-date, other than standard officer/director compensation and indemnification arrangements) |
| Loans / advances / guarantees to related parties | None disclosed | — | — | — | — | FY2026 DEF 14A, same section |
| Royalty / brand / management fees to promoter group | Not applicable — no promoter/controlling group exists | — | — | — | — | FY2026 DEF 14A; `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` (no controlling promoter) |
| Rent / other payments to promoter entities | Not applicable | — | — | — | — | FY2026 DEF 14A |
| Outstanding receivables from related parties | Not disclosed | — | — | — | — | FY2026 DEF 14A |

Uber discloses a written Related Party Transactions Policy requiring Audit Committee pre-approval for any transaction above $120,000 involving a director, executive officer, or holder of more than 5% of any class of stock, evaluated against an arm's-length standard [FY2026 DEF 14A, "Policies & Procedures for Transactions with Related Persons," p.34]. Under that policy, the only "Other Transactions" the FY2026 proxy actually itemizes are standard equity awards (options/RSUs to officers and directors) and change-in-control severance arrangements, both covered in the Compensation Discussion & Analysis section, and the filing states these are "comparable to terms we could have obtained in arm's-length dealings with unrelated third parties." **No material related-party transaction (commercial contract, loan, guarantee, or lease) with PIF, any director, or any 5%+ holder is disclosed anywhere in the FY2026 proxy.** This is a materially cleaner picture than the "cannot assess" caveat the pool-only triage (`00_governance-data-triage.md`) had to carry forward — the primary filing closes that gap directly: no RPT above threshold is disclosed, which is itself the finding, not an absence of evidence. One Section 16(a) filing-compliance footnote: Nikesh Arora's initial Form 3 inadvertently omitted indirectly-held shares and was corrected via an amended filing — an administrative lapse, not a substantive RPT or self-dealing issue [FY2026 DEF 14A, "Delinquent Section 16(a) Reports," p.33].

## 4. Takeover Defenses & Voting Rights

| Feature | Present? | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Poison pill | No | Explicitly listed under "What We Don't Do": "Have a stockholder rights plan (poison pill)" | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Corporate Governance Highlights," p.11 |
| Classified / staggered board | No | All directors stand for annual election; explicitly listed as "What We Don't Do: Have a classified board" | FY2026 DEF 14A, p.11 |
| Dual-class / unequal voting | No | Explicitly listed as "What We Don't Do: Dual class stock" — a single class of common stock, one vote per share | FY2026 DEF 14A, p.11 |
| Majority vs plurality director voting | Majority | Directors elected by majority of votes cast in uncontested elections (an incumbent who fails to receive a majority must tender a resignation for Board evaluation); no supermajority vote is required to amend bylaws/charter or to remove directors | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Corporate Governance Highlights," p.11; "Election of Directors," p.20 |
| Shareholder ability to call meetings / act by written consent | Partial | Stockholders holding at least 25% of the aggregate voting power (and meeting other bylaw requirements) may call a special meeting. Proxy access is available to a holder or group of up to 20 holders continuously owning ≥3% of voting stock for 3+ years, entitled to nominate up to the greater of 2 directors or 20% of the board. No shareholder right to act by written consent in lieu of a meeting is disclosed — stockholder action is limited to a duly called meeting | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Corporate Governance Highlights," p.11 |

**Read:** on the standard takeover-defense checklist, Uber's charter and bylaws are shareholder-friendly by design — no pill, no classified board, no dual class, no supermajority requirements, majority voting. The one real limit on shareholder power is procedural: the 25% ownership threshold to call a special meeting is a real (not nominal) bar for a company with a market capitalization near $140 billion and a 99.7%-institutionally-held float — no single holder other than Vanguard (9.34%) is anywhere close to that threshold alone, so calling a special meeting in practice requires several large institutions to coordinate. No written-consent right compounds that: shareholders cannot force a vote between annual meetings without either that 25% coalition or management's own cooperation.

## 4A. Shareholder Voting & Dilution

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| AGM votes against (remuneration / RPT / director / auditor resolutions) | Say-on-Pay support has run "approximately 85% or higher" every year for the past five years, averaging ~91% in favor — no evidence of high (>20%) opposition on pay, and no RPT resolution exists to vote on (none proposed). Individual director-election vote tallies for the 2026 meeting are not yet available (meeting is scheduled for May 4, 2026, after this proxy's filing date; this report is dated Aug-06-2026, after that meeting, but the actual 5.07/8-K vote-count filing for the 2026 AGM was not located in this search) | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Say-on-Pay Results & Stockholder Engagement," p.44 |
| Proxy-advisor recommendations (ISS / Glass Lewis / IiAS) | Not disclosed in the proxy itself and not located in the data pool; the ~91% five-year average Say-on-Pay support is consistent with, but not direct proof of, favorable proxy-advisor recommendations | Not proven from available data |
| Institutional opposition to any resolution | No specific instance of institutional opposition is disclosed in the FY2026 proxy; the Section 16(a) Arora Form 3 correction is an administrative filing lapse, not a voting-opposition event | FY2026 DEF 14A, p.33 |
| Dilution history (preferential allotment, warrants, QIP, ESOP pool) | Shares outstanding rose modestly from 2,042,560,121 (per the CIQ pool, as of the Aug-2026 quote date) to 2,042,946,343 (per the DEF 14A, as of March 2, 2026) — a small net change reflecting ongoing RSU vesting and stock-based compensation offset by buybacks; `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` documents share count falling from 2,089mm (Dec-2024) to 2,036mm (Mar-2026) on ~$6.5–6.9bn/year buybacks before ticking back up to 2,043mm (Aug-2026) as buybacks were throttled to fund the Delivery Hero stake purchase | FY2026 DEF 14A, "Security Ownership...," p.80; `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`, Historical Capitalization tab |

No high-votes-against pattern is evident on the metrics available (Say-on-Pay). Individual director-election and auditor-ratification vote counts for the actual 2026 AGM are not available in the sources reviewed — a genuine residual gap, since this proxy is the *notice* for that meeting, not the results.

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

Uber's board is genuinely independent by the numbers that matter — 90% independent directors, a fully independent chair separate from the CEO, 100%-independent Audit/Comp/Nominating committees, majority voting, no poison pill, no classified board, and no dual-class stock, all confirmed from the FY2026 DEF 14A rather than inferred from a vendor export. Related-party transactions are a genuine non-issue on the evidence: the proxy discloses a working Audit-Committee-gated RPT policy and reports zero disclosable transactions above the $120,000 threshold. The single biggest structural gap for a minority holder is procedural, not personnel-based: shareholders cannot act between annual meetings except through a 25%-of-voting-power coalition to call a special meeting (no written-consent right exists), which in a 99.7%-institutionally-held, no-single-holder-above-10% register is a real, not theoretical, constraint on rapid shareholder response to a crisis. Layered on top is the PIF-linked Audit Committee seat — disclosed, not a hidden RPT channel, but a sovereign-wealth-fund executive overseeing financial-reporting integrity while his fund holds a top-five stake is a fact minority holders should track, not ignore. On balance this reads as **adequate** protection: strong on-paper board independence and clean related-party discipline, offset by a real (if procedural, not personal) limit on shareholders' ability to force action outside the annual cycle.

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — UBER

**Data-pool caveat.** No DEF 14A, 10-K, 10-Q, 8-K, or shareholder letter is in `data/UBER/`. What IS present, and used here: one **verbatim** earnings-call transcript (Q2 FY2026, Aug-05-2026, S&P Global Market Intelligence transcript of the live call — full trust applies to the words themselves), a multi-year Capital IQ guidance-vs-actual history (2019–2026), and the cross-module `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` (non-GAAP and accrual read, available — no cross-check gap) and `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` (beat/miss pattern). **Limitation that matters for this specific agent:** the one verbatim quarter in the pool happens to be a strong quarter (Adjusted EBITDA beat, record free cash flow) — it is NOT the worst recent quarter. FQ3 2025 and FQ4 2025 (Adjusted EPS misses of −25.4% and −9.6% vs. consensus) are the actual weak prints in this window, and no transcript for either exists in the pool. Section 4 below is therefore built on how management handled the negative sub-plots embedded WITHIN an otherwise good quarter (a soft revenue print, a margin-optics reclassification, a broken capital-return framework, competitive softness in Brazil) — a genuine but imperfect proxy for "tone in bad times," not a direct read of the actual worst quarter. Confidence on every transcript-sourced row is held at 3, not 5, for this reason.

## 1. Owning the Misses

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Revenue missed Street's own consensus two quarters running (FQ1 2026 −0.45%, FQ2 2026 −0.52%) | Not proactively addressed anywhere in the Q2 FY2026 call — no analyst asked about the revenue-consensus miss directly, and neither Khosrowshahi nor Krishnamurthy raised it. Because Uber stopped issuing formal Revenue guidance in FQ2 2020, there is no "guidance miss" to own — but the metric now under the most pressure is also the one management does not discuss unprompted. | **Neither owned nor spun — silence.** This is a disclosure choice (what to guide, what to volunteer), not active obscuring, but it is not candor either. | `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` §6; Q2 FY2026 earnings call transcript (full text reviewed, no mention found) |
| ~50% of free cash flow to buybacks framework overridden — $4bn pulled from repurchases in Q2 2026 to fund the Delivery Hero stake | Directly asked by an analyst (Mahaney, Evercore) and answered with a specific number, a specific reason, and a specific timeline: "we have bought back about $3.5 billion of our stock this year... we tactically pivoted quite heavily towards M&A in the second quarter. We deployed about $4 billion of capital... Now that we are through that announcement, we are steadily looking to rebuild our share repurchase levels... I don't think this is a year away. We are talking about months, not quarters." | **Owned — direct, quantified, dated.** | Q2 FY2026 earnings call transcript, Q&A, p.11-12 (CFO Balaji Krishnamurthy) |
| U.K. Mobility take-rate reclassification — ~400bps of a ~500bps YoY take-rate decline is presentation, not economics | Directly explained by the CFO without evasion: "remember that we have talked about a business model change in the U.K... Of that 500 basis points, about 400 basis points is entirely related to this U.K. business model change, and it's an optical impact. It moves cost from cost of revenue... what I would ask investors to focus on is not necessarily the take rate we're reporting on revenues, but the net sort of take rate that is disclosed in our 10-Q filing... mobility operating income margin remains very strong at 7.6%." | **Owned — quantified, named the true comparability metric, redirected to a harder number (7.6% operating margin).** | Q2 FY2026 earnings call transcript, Q&A, p.12 (CFO); `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` §5 |
| Brazil mobility competitive softness (trip growth pressured by DiDi/Meituan food-delivery expansion bidding up 2-wheeler supply) | Directly addressed by the CEO on a direct question, with mechanism explained (delivery competitors bidding up 2-wheeler driver supply, forcing Uber to shift incentives), though without a specific $ or trip-count magnitude: "it is affecting trip volumes and it's actually competition outside of our space that is affecting those trip volumes." | **Owned — mechanism disclosed, magnitude not quantified.** | Q2 FY2026 earnings call transcript, Q&A, p.8 (CEO, responding to Doug Anmuth, JPMorgan) |
| "A track record of being disciplined on headcount addition over multiple years" — no supporting time series in the data pool | Restated when directly challenged (Colantuoni, Jefferies, asked what specifically allowed headcount moderation); the CFO answered with AI-productivity anecdotes (near-100% engineer adoption of AI coding tools, "a doubling in the code output per engineer") and one concrete data point (10-20% headcount cuts "in a couple of organizations" this quarter), but never produced the headcount trend the claim rests on. | **Partially owned — a real, specific answer was given, but the underlying claim remains unverifiable from any document in this pool.** | Q2 FY2026 earnings call transcript, Q&A, p.9 (CFO) |

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring "one-off" addbacks | Two distinct recurring items function as "one-off, but happened again": (1) a deferred-tax valuation-allowance release inflated GAAP net income by $5,758mm (FY2024) and $4,346mm (FY2025) — the same non-cash mechanism, two years running, each larger than roughly half that year's reported net income; (2) ~$592mm (FY2025) / ~$630mm (LTM) of Adjusted EBITDA's own add-back — beyond stock-based compensation — recurs every period with its composition never itemized in this pool. | `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` §4, §5, §8 |
| GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend) | Adj. EBITDA exceeds GAAP-based EBITDA (Operating Income + D&A) by +38.3% in FY2025 ($8,730mm vs. $6,312mm). Stock-based compensation ($1,826mm, ~3.5% of FY2025 revenue) is the largest single addback and is disclosed; the remaining ~25% of the gap is not broken out by line item anywhere in the pool. | `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` §4, §7 |
| Does adjusted framing flatter reality? | Mixed, not uniformly aggressive. On Adjusted EBITDA, yes — the metric strips out a real, dilutive cost (SBC) and roughly a quarter of the total gap is opaque. But on the company's OWN net income/EPS normalization, the direction runs the opposite way from the usual non-GAAP pattern: Uber's Normalized Net Income ($3,613mm FY2025) is **lower** than GAAP net income ($10,053mm) because management strips out the one-time tax benefit and investment mark-to-market gains that flatter the GAAP number — a genuine, if partial, candor signal, since most companies' "adjusted" figure flatters rather than discounts the reported one. | `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` §7, §9 |

**Cross-module reconciliation:** `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` scores Earnings Quality at 68/100 ("mostly clean but some working capital or adjustment noise"), driven by cash-backed earnings that are genuinely strong (CFO/Adj. EBITDA at 115.7% in FY2025) sitting alongside a GAAP EPS series distorted by the deferred-tax release and volatile mark-to-market gains. This module's candor read is consistent with that: the cash-flow disclosure is trustworthy; the GAAP-EPS-as-growth-narrative is not, and nothing in the Q2 FY2026 transcript proactively flags that distortion to investors — it is visible only by cross-referencing the balance sheet (Deferred Tax Assets, LT: +$6,001mm FY2024, +$4,780mm FY2025) against the income statement, work this module's earnings agent did, not work management volunteered on the call.

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Segment-level economics that matter ARE disclosed with real numbers when management chooses to volunteer them — the U.K. take-rate reclassification (Section 1) came with an exact basis-point breakdown and a redirect to the true operating-margin number (7.6%), and the CFO named the specific mechanics of the $10bn AV investment program (equity stakes vs. offtake/vehicle-purchase commitments, "$2.50 of external capital raised for every $1 Uber invests"). But two things got LESS transparent over time, not more: Uber stopped guiding Revenue in 2020 — the exact line now missing Street consensus — and the pool contains no compensation-metric or related-party-transaction disclosure at all (a proxy-statement gap, not addressed on any call). Neither of these is a call-tone problem; both are structural disclosure gaps that predate this quarter and were not remedied by it.

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

The only verbatim quarter available is a good one (Adjusted EBITDA and EPS both beat guidance, record trailing FCF), so this section reads tone against the negative sub-plots inside a strong print, not against an actual weak quarter — a real limitation, stated up front. Within that constraint, the tone read is candid, not evasive: every negative or complicating item raised by an analyst (the buyback pause, the U.K. margin optics, Brazil competition) got a direct, numbers-first answer rather than a deflection to a different topic, and the CFO used the phrase "optical impact" about Uber's own reported take-rate rather than letting the improved-looking number stand unexplained. The one soft spot is the CEO's Delivery Hero framing — "we believe this deal will create significant long-term shareholder value" — asserted without a quantified return metric (no stated IRR, payback period, or ROIC target anywhere in the call), which is the closest this transcript comes to boilerplate.

## 5. Candor Read

Management answers direct questions about bad news with real numbers rather than talking around them — the $4bn buyback diversion, the U.K. take-rate's "optical" 400bps, and Brazil's competitive dynamics were all owned with specifics, not spin — and the company's own Normalized Net Income runs more conservative than GAAP, the opposite of the usual non-GAAP inflation pattern. Set against that: Uber stopped guiding the one line item now missing consensus, roughly a quarter of its own Adjusted EBITDA addback is never itemized, and a non-cash deferred-tax benefit has now inflated GAAP net income by a combined ~$10 billion over two straight years with no management commentary flagging the distortion to anyone not cross-checking the balance sheet — plus the evidence base here is thin, one good-quarter transcript, not a tested response to an actual bad print. No unresolved adverse integrity signal was routed from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` or `01_management-and-track-record` (RF-MGT-005 not triggered), so there is no compounding integrity concern weighing on this read. The single biggest candor signal is the U.K. take-rate call: management could have let an "improving" reported number stand uncorrected and chose instead to name it as optical and redirect to the harder metric — real evidence of trustworthiness — but it sits next to a genuinely opaque quarter of Adjusted EBITDA addbacks that management has never itemized on any call in this pool. Verdict: **mixed**.

RF-DISC-002 (recurring "one-off" / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs)
